And beyond preterm birth, high temperatures are also associated with stillbirths, with low birth weight, with some birth defects, and also with hypertensive disorders like preeclampsia in the mother, which can be fatal. So researchers have established a definite connection between heat and risks in pregnancy to the child and to the mother. Yes, they have. But what they don't have yet is an understanding of the biological mechanisms by which that happens. Because also all of these studies are based just on the statistics of temperatures and statistics of stuff like preterm birth. And it's really important to know why this kind of heat exposure affects pregnancies in this way, because then you can actually start tailoring your interventions in response. But researching that is incredibly difficult because you have to monitor women all throughout their pregnancies. You have to track very carefully both how their health changes and how the health of the baby changes. And you also have to track the temperatures that they are exposed to the whole time. And for this kind of work to really make a difference, it also has to be done in the places and with the people who are being most affected at the moment, which means the people in the poorest countries. I was in Pakistan, where the largest scale study into this kind of biological mechanism is going on. And the logistics were mind-boggling. So they're gonna have 6,000 pregnant women in this cohort by the end. They've only just started, so they've currently got 400. They are from three different locations. So one is the kind of outskirts of Karachi. One is Mitari, which is a sort of rural, agricultural village setting. And then the last one is Miti, which is a city in the desert. And for each of those women that they have currently got, they have to find them before their 13th week of pregnancy, which is difficult given that a lot of them would not normally disclose it or seek medical attention at that point. The way that they do that is that there are sort of government and NGO-funded lady health workers, they're called, who will sort of check in on villages and talk to women about their health. Once they've done that, they have to persuade them to come in for incredibly detailed sonograms and blood tests, despite quite pervasive myths that detailed scans are harmful to the baby. A lot of these women are not allowed to go places on their own. And so they have to have their transport organized to and from everything, also with a chaperone. But you're organizing all of this in contexts where you don't have phone signal a lot of the time, or you're with people who do not have phones. They have to wear a temperature sensor 24-7. There's another subset that has to have a sample of their placenta collected 30 minutes after birth. So, you know, you're organizing all of this, and it will take five years, but the kind of findings from this size of study should be really, really helpful at working out exactly at which points this heat exposure may result in a bad outcome. And when we're talking about heat affecting pregnant women, are we just talking about really hot places like Pakistan? No, we're not at all. And as I said earlier, it's to do with the amount of heat that your body is acclimatized to. That said, it is worse currently in the hottest, poorest places, because A, temperatures are really very high, and B, you don't have a lot of access to healthcare. You also don't have a lot of options to be out of the heat. You know, in places like the Gambia, women are 50% of the agricultural workforce, and they will work throughout their entire pregnancies. And they're really, really exposed. But you see this in every country in the world. And you also, within countries, see it being worse along the lines of existing inequalities. So in the Californian study in 2010, the risk of a preterm birth went up more than double for black mothers than for white mothers, even if they were living in the same area and were exposed to the same kind of temperatures. So how bad could it get? There are no unified global statistics on this, because in part, governments do not track heat and health outcomes. But there is modelling that has been done in several countries. And so, for example, in China, they think that over 13,000 preterm births a year are caused by heat waves, and that at least a quarter of them over the last couple of decades has been caused by climate change. And then that starts rising, and it's about a third after about 2015. And then that is obviously going to rise as temperatures increase. And some modelling that was recently done by the London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine looked at Kenya and South Africa in a factual scenario, so what we actually have now, and a counterfactual scenario in what would happen in a world that hit net zero by 2050 and were at 1.5 degrees of temperature rise at the end of the century, both of which we are so far off track to do. In both Kenya and South Africa, even if we cut emissions so rapidly, you would A, see child mortality reducing much more slowly than it otherwise would, and you would also see preterm births rising by at least a fifth. And then in the modelling for the trajectory that we're actually on, those numbers go up to preterm births rising by 50%. So it is then really, really significant. And often when we talk about climate change, we're thinking about impacts that will happen in the future, possibly for other generations. They can seem kind of vague and far off. Indian streets over the past, let's say a decade or two decades, have seen an influx of automobiles. In order to ease congestion, what city authorities tend to do is they widen roads. We've seen some effort to improve the generosity of pensions. So what's going on with pensions? I would normally think of pensions as being synonymous with saving, not stimulus. So there's this rising chorus of economists and government advisors who think that pension reform could be good stimulus policy. So it's not just social policy. It's not just about making people feel more comfortable in their retirement. It's also good for the macroeconomy. So the hope is that if you make pensions more generous, the people who are receiving the pensions will spend more. But even better, the younger people who are looking forward to their pensions will be a little less concerned to save. They'll count instead on the pension safety net to tide them over during their retirement, and they won't be quite so determined to build a separate nest egg. One former government advisor has said, if you spend a trillion yuan on rural pensions, you'll increase GDP by about 1.2 trillion, and that could be quite useful right now. So Simon, let's just step back for a minute. What does the pension system look like at the moment? Why is it not working to do that? So China's pension system is often described as having three pillars. There's the personal, the occupational, and the public. That makes it sound very tidy, like a piece of classical architecture, but actually it's much messier than that. It's a whole variety of schemes stitched together. So the broadest scheme covering rural residents and people in the cities who aren't working is not very generous, but also doesn't make huge demands on people. Then there's a more extensive scheme built around employers in urban areas, where both employees and their employers make contributions. On top of that, enterprises can offer their own schemes, and then you can also take out your personal pension if you want. So the government's been tinkering with all of these schemes. In particular, it wants the occupational schemes to invest a bit more adventurously. At the moment, a lot of the money is flowing into really quite safe investments, and the reason for that is that the people who are managing the money tend to be judged year by year. So there's no real concept of investing in stocks for the long run. They basically have to invest in things that aren't going to lose money in any single year. The government wants to change that and have them evaluated over a three-year or even longer span. That hopefully will improve returns to these schemes. And what else are they doing about this? For the urban scheme that covers workers, the big initiative there is to try and extend the net to include all of the gig workers that China now has. Often those more flexible forms of employment fall through the net of the existing pension schemes. And then the rural scheme, they've become a little bit more generous. So in the last couple of years, China has offered a 20 yuan increase in the monthly payout. And that sounds like terribly little, and indeed it is. It's actually quite large compared with previous increases. So at least there's a sort of recognition that that has to become more generous. Simon, the big ambition here is to make people feel more secure so that they spend rather than save, right? Are these measures enough, or what else could they be doing? So I think the most important thing they could do is make the rural pension much more generous. And I'm not talking about a 20 yuan increase. They need to triple the size of these pensions to really make a difference to rural spending. That would be affordable. It costs about half a percent of GDP, and it would make a big difference in the lives of the people receiving it now, but also a big difference to the outlook of people who could expect to receive it in the future. I think it would help to lower their savings rate. And a lot of people in China who live on these pensions are still quite young, quite active. Until recently, you could retire at the age of 50. If you were a woman in a blue-collar job, those retirement ages are creeping up. But people still have the capacity to spend. And indeed, some economists have noticed that it tends to be the older parts of the country that in many ways are a little bit more exuberant right now, who are spending a little bit more. And it's the youth who are unemployed and depressed and lying flat. So there is some consumption potential there. And another key reform would be for the government to move more of its stakes in state-owned enterprises into the pension pot. So China has these enormous state-owned enterprises. Many of them are really quite profitable. And it would be good if pensioners, the public, had a more direct claim on those profits. Right now, China's economy is suffering from such a chronic shortfall of demand, such a shortfall of spending. It really has to do something to make this large cohort of the country more secure and more willing to open their wallets. Simon, thank you so much. Thank you. Earlier this year, there was a pretty controversial advert for Audible, an audiobooks platform, which discussed the chosen speeds at which people listen to their audiobooks. Lizzie Peet is a researcher for The Economist. Hollywood icon Glenn Powell likes listening at one-time speed. I want to hear people talk at a normal human rate. Bowen Yang, an American comedian, on the other hand, prefers a faster-paced narration. You can round up to two, but you need 1.8, which is, I think, is what Goldilocks also likes. This advert sparked a fierce online debate, with some feeling it implied that those who make haste are weird. I think people who go real fast are, I don't want to say psychopath, but it's giving that. Some claim to feel shamed by the ad and threaten to cancel their subscriptions. Lizzie, do many people listen to podcasts speeded up? I've heard anecdotally that a lot of people are listening to podcasts and audiobooks sped up just to get through the sheer avalanche of content there is out there. Hang on, I'm not following. Tell me about this at normal speed. I've heard anecdotally that a lot of people are listening to podcasts and audiobooks sped up just to get through the sheer avalanche of content there is out there. So The Economist asked YouGov to do a poll of this, and it came back with some quite startling results. 31% of Americans between the ages of 18 and 29 listen to audio at playback speeds faster than one. And the figure for those aged 45 and over is just 8%. Apple and Spotify offer users the option to speed through their podcasts. Netflix also has a button that you can toggle the speed on certain formats. YouTube has a similar feature and actually recently rolled out even a 4X option to its premier subscribers. Do you do this, Lizzie? Well, it kind of depends for me on what exactly I'm listening to. I mean, I would obviously never speed you up, Rosie. But there are some podcasts these days that go on for sort of hours, and I personally just don't really have that time, even if I want to listen to the content. But obviously for the audio and video platforms, there's not really a downside to offering this feature. The more books and episodes that someone can get through, the more advertising and sales revenue come in. And in terms of time saved, YouTube recently published some statistics where they claim that viewers save more than 900 years per day by toggling the playback speed above 1. We did a back-of-the-envelope calculation. If you had a 10-hour car journey, you could listen to Persuasion Unabridged in 8 hours and 13 minutes if you listened at normal speed. But if you click that just to 1.5 speed, so not even double speed, that would go down to 5 hours and 30 minutes, after which you could fit in several other shorter books. But does that really work? I mean, can you really take in what people are saying when they're sped up? You've hit on the instinctive reaction that a lot of people have to this, which is this image in your head of people's brains being frazzled by endless content coming in at faster and faster pace. There's not a huge amount of studies that have been published on the exact impact of speed on concentration. But those that have indicate that it doesn't seem to have that much of an effect on the listener's ability to concentrate and to process information. So I spoke to Marcus Pearce, who's a cognitive scientist, and he pointed out that the average person speaks at a rate of 150 words per minute. But most brains are capable of processing information much, much faster than that. The best studies have actually looked at the impact on students who speed up video lectures. And there have been a lot of studies testing what happens when students sped up the rate at which their lectures were played back and then took a test on the content of the lecture. And overall, they found that up to 1.5 speed, there isn't really much difference in performance. That changes quite significantly as you approach and exceed double speed. But I was even surprised that just going up to 1.5 speed didn't seem to have that much of an effect, which I think is the main takeaway from all of this. Isn't that all quite transactional, though? I mean, I can sort of see it with news. You might be trying to get information. But enjoying a podcast is also about enjoying the experience. It's an entertainment. You know, we put quite a lot of work into pacing that kind of thing. Surely all of that's lost when you're on fast forward. Completely understandable. All of this really boils down to a matter of taste. It's up to you personally what you choose to speed up and what you don't. Someone I spoke to compared this to skim reading. So a lot of people skim read books just to get through them a bit faster. There are techniques that came about in the 1950s and 60s of people that could train their brains to scan pages at a much faster rate, almost up to 30 pages a minute. But to your point, Rosie, actors, audio book narrators and podcast presenters often rely on rhythm and pauses for emphasis and meaning. Few actors, for example, would race through soliloquies in Hamlet. At the end of the day, it really comes down to a question of habit and personal taste. It's fundamentally up to you where you choose to draw that line. Fascinating and a bit depressing. Thank you, Lizzie. Thanks very much, Rosie. Transcription by ESO. And in Florida, you can get paid 50 bucks for the first four feet, and 25 for every foot after that. Bricklaying? Rope-making? Nope. Python hunting. The snakes are ravaging other wildlife in the Everglades, and the state is gamifying their capture. But first… Climate change is affecting Africa in many ways. Not only are temperatures rising, the weather's growing more extreme, droughts are getting worse, floods and cyclones are growing more intense. Robert Guest is a deputy editor at The Economist. And all this will change how diseases spread. One of the most worrying is malaria, which is transmitted by mosquitoes and currently kills more than half a million people a year, mostly African children. I spoke to an entomologist, Pawar Shikai, who's based in KwaZulu-Natal, a province of South Africa. When it comes to mosquitoes, climate change can be a double-edged sword. It can increase the mosquitoes. The mosquitoes can also expand their ranges. But it can also make the environment inhospitable to the mosquitoes. But in our case, we noticed that the frequency of floods would work for the mosquitoes, because then they will have more breeding grounds. At the moment, malaria is more or less being kept under control in South Africa. But with changing weather patterns, that will get harder. And it's not just South Africa. Countries across the continent are having to adapt to deal with the changing geography of a deadly disease. And I suppose it's not just that it's deadly, but that it's also costly. Absolutely. Most people who get malaria don't die of it, but it's extremely unpleasant. A female mosquito bites you in order to drink blood, and while doing that, injects a parasite into you, which multiplies and it gives you a fever and chills. It's not just very unpleasant. Feverish people find it harder to work. Children learn less at school when they're sick. And when children are taken to the clinic to be treated, it's almost entirely the mothers who have to put down whatever they were doing, stop work, stop earning money in order to go and do it, and then there's a long wait. So a recent study found that a 10% fall in malaria incidence was associated with an increase in incomes of a third of a percentage point. So that's significant. If you could reduce the burden of malaria, there would be more money to use for everything, including development. But instead, you're saying climate change might not reduce, but increase that burden. So at the most basic level, mosquitoes thrive in warm weather. And so as the climate warms, they're going to move into more places where the population aren't necessarily going to have resistance to malaria. There's an estimate from something called the Malaria Atlas Project, an NGO, that by 2050, 1.3 billion Africans will live in areas where malaria will be harder to eradicate than it is today because of climate change. If you add it all up, the Malaria Atlas Project reckons there'll be an extra half a million deaths or so cumulatively by 2050 because of climate change, unless serious action is taken to alleviate it. And all of that is essentially down to more places being amenable for mosquitoes because of changing temperatures. It's more complicated than that. Warmer weather could make the malaria seasons longer. It could speed up the mosquitoes' life cycle. It could push the disease into areas where people are less used to it and have less resistance. It could even make people reluctant to use bed nets because they make you hot. But the big thing is that it's making natural disasters, things like floods and cyclones, more common and more severe. So talk me through why that makes matters more severe. You look at a place like Mozambique, which is particularly vulnerable to cyclones and floods. They had floods in December where 70,000 homes were destroyed and about half a million people were affected. So what happens is when the floodwaters recede, they leave stagnant pools and puddles where mosquitoes can breed. You find that people are displaced from their homes, which are probably being doused on the inside with insecticide. And that means they're more likely to get bitten. And then when people get sick, it's harder to reach the clinic because the roads have been washed away. So people are more likely to die. The Malaria Atlas Project projects that probably 90% of the extra malaria deaths are going to come from extreme weather. And of course, you can't plan for those events, but you can know that they're coming, that they're more likely. What matters of preparation are there? Key is going to be knowing that this is more likely to happen and preparing. So you can, for example, rather than keeping all the supplies of malaria drugs and bed nets in a central place and distributing them normally, you've got to pre-position those supplies in more places, disperse them around the country so that people are more likely to be close to them, to have access to them, even when disaster strikes. There are other more technical things like trying to make malaria drugs more heat stable so they don't spoil. There's also really interesting work being done with data to try to make sure that you combine the climate data and the malaria data to have a better sense of how the disease is changing in its geographical spread and to predict in future where you're going to have more of a problem. You're describing national responses, but much like climate change itself, malaria doesn't know about borders. What about when the new areas where malaria is shifting to, it crosses a border? There's a very good example of that I went to have a look at in South Africa and Mozambique. So Mozambique, much poorer, much more malarial. They had maybe 9 million cases in 2023, whereas South Africa, which has a cooler, drier climate and much more sophisticated mosquito bashing techniques, only had about 5,000. So there's a huge difference in incidence between the two countries. But there's also quite a lot of movement between them. South Africa has higher wages. Migrants want to come to South Africa and work. And what the health authorities have realised in South Africa is that it would be pointless to say we're just going to stop anyone with malaria coming into the country. You can't do that. They've got thousands of kilometres of essentially undefended borders. People would get through anyway. I went to the bus station near the border to see the mobile malaria clinic and talked to some of the people who work there, including Jolie Mokise, who's an environmental health practitioner. One of the positive cases that we took this morning. OK. So what does a negative case look like? These are negative. And how do we know they're negative? Because it's one line? It's one line. So one line on the plastic. It looks a bit like a COVID test, doesn't it? Yeah. OK. So one line is negative. And what does the positive one look like? This is a positive one from this morning. It's a faint positive line. We've also got very clear positives. But we treat every case as a positive. As long as there's a faint line on there, we treat it as a positive test. Jolie explained to me how the team encourages people coming across the border to get tested. So a mobiliser will come in, introduce himself and say, there's a tent, the blue tent right there for you to come and test for malaria. You are testing for free. You'll get medication for free. And you take the medication for three days. That also pervades them more. Then they realise it's not a chronic medication that they'll have to take for life. Once they realise three days of medication, it's likely that they'll come in, they'll give us the information and they'll move right along. Crucially, they're allowed to keep travelling. The clinics don't ask what your visa status is or anything like that. You can keep going. But you get the free medicine and it makes it much less likely that you're going to pass it on. And that is probably a very effective way of keeping the extra malaria out. And a lot more countries are going to have to think about that in the future. Everything we've been talking about so far is either about treatment or about diagnosis. What about prevention? There's a lot you can do for prevention from a personal level, wearing long sleeves, insect repellent, trying not to get bitten. But the most exciting stuff happening at the moment is vaccination. People have been working on trying to develop a malaria vaccine for decades. And it's only recently that we've had some really promising leads. There are now two new vaccines which seem to prevent around half of cases in children under five. There were pilots they did in Ghana, Kenya and Malawi which showed a 13% drop in overall child mortality among the populations who were taking them. So now these are being aggressively rolled out to children in about 20 countries so far. That's very encouraging. And although the parasite will evolve resistance at some point, there's going to be an arms race there. And the drug companies are doing better than they have done previously. There are other quite clever things that can be done, like sterilising male mosquitoes. If you zap them with gamma rays to make their sperm infertile, as Paweł Szykaj told me. It's releasing sterilised males into the wild so that they mate with the females. And the females would actually collect sperms that are sterile. And even when they lay eggs, they will lay eggs that are infertile. And they will not hatch. So by doing so, you're reducing the population density. And how do you make a mosquito? The males are all sterilised in a laboratory. And then they're taken in what's called a mosquito taxi. It's a van which is used to transport really large numbers of them. They're taken in this van under temperature control conditions. And then they're released. So the grim sounding estimates that you started with something on the order of half a million extra deaths by 2050 from malaria, that future might be avoided from the sound of things. Those estimates are what is likely to happen if we don't step up our efforts. But like many things with climate change, there are ways that we could step up our efforts. There will be new ways discovered of doing so. But there's an urgency to start thinking about it and to start planning for it now. Robert, thanks very much for joining us. Jason, thank you. I was recently rammed by a delivery driver in China. I was pulling into my office parking lot and suddenly I got smashed from behind. Don Wineland is the Economist's China Business and Finance Editor. This guy on a motorcycle had crashed into my back tire. One of the reasons why it's not too surprising right now to get into an accident with a driver or to see one happen on the streets in a city like Shanghai or Beijing, is because there's so many drivers on the roads. And that's because China's big tech companies have entered into something of a delivery war. And the war is simply because it's bigger tech companies that have, well, entered or started the war. That's right. So in February this year, JD.com, this is one of China's biggest e-commerce groups, said that it would start offering food delivery services. Now, food delivery services are the territory of another very, very big company called Meituan. There's also a group called Alibaba that has its own delivery service. So when JD.com said it would start delivering food, it was really entering into a battle with these two other large tech companies. And since then, they've been fighting it out for the delivery business in China. The food delivery market in China is not small. Last year, it was worth 1.6 trillion yuan or 220 billion dollars. The way that these companies are fighting out this war is by pushing prices on their applications lower and lower and lower. So right now, you can get a coffee or a tea for just one yuan. That's, you know, a couple cents. You can also buy a smartphone and have it delivered to your house in about 30 minutes. As you might imagine, this isn't great for the companies that actually sell these things. Small restaurants or shops that sell tea or coffee. When the tech companies push down the prices, it's not them that's covering all of those costs. It is the vendors that are also having to make up this difference in price. So, yes, there are a lot more deliveries. People are getting a lot more stuff. But it's really squeezing all sorts of different companies across China. People are getting a lot more stuff and they're getting it cheaper, right? So that's something. Yeah, you would think that there is a winner here somewhere. But customers probably aren't getting more and more quality out of this. They might be getting some things for a lot cheaper. But if you think about the amount of money restaurants now have to spend on making a pork fried rice or something like that, they have less and less. So, as a consumer in this country, I do have to think, if I'm only paying a restaurant 3 yuan for something, what can I possibly be getting? It can't be high quality. You might also think that delivery drivers are making out pretty well out of this. There's more of them employed now by these tech companies. And they're getting more orders. But from what we can tell, they're making less and less per order. So in order to make the same amount of money that they were making before, they have to do more orders. That just means more people on the streets, driving faster and faster. It doesn't really sound very good for these drivers. It's also not good for the people around them that are on bikes, like myself. So I don't see a lot of good coming out for customers or for drivers. So the spoils of the war then are just accruing to those tech giants. You would think that the tech giants would be cashing in on this. But that's also not the case, surprisingly. They are having to burn some of their cash in order to subsidize the prices. So by one estimate, JD.com's delivery service is going to lose 33 billion yuan this year. That's massive. All of these companies stand to lose on their delivery services this year. Or at least see thinner profits. One thing that's quite important about these companies is their reputations in China. Recently, people have been posting unflattering remarks about Meituan online. So there's this phrase that's used to describe the cruelty of the Qing dynasty. It goes something like, life under the Qing is suffering. But people have been putting Meituan in the place of Qing. That's quite telling about where these companies stand in terms of their reputations. So to hear you tell it, nobody is winning. Why are the big tech companies carrying on in this way, though, if even their reputations are on the line? Is it just hoping that they'll choke off the competition and then they'll be the last ones standing? Yeah, I think in a way this is a classic price war. So usually in this kind of war, you do see everyone losing out. But this also is quite reflective of what's going on more broadly in China. People often talk about relentless, meaningless competition. Where you have hundreds of companies competing for the same thing. And essentially everyone is kind of running themselves into the ground. There's a word that they use for this. It's called involution. And it's kind of come to define the last couple years in China. Everyone is fighting for the same market and prices are falling. You see this across a number of different industries. In the auto industry, there's also a very intense price war going on. So intense, in fact, that many of the large auto manufacturers can't really pay their suppliers. Because they don't have enough income. One estimate says that about 80% of industrial sectors are reporting negative price growth right now compared to last year. That does tell you something about the shape of the Chinese economy. I mean, in China, that sounds like a great time for the strong hand of the state to come in. Yeah, you would think that the state would come in and do something. And they are starting to. The central government, the Communist Party, has been indicating recently that they want to end involution. On July 24th, a couple regulators got together and they said that they wanted to change China's price law. And the law that they proposed would probably stop the large tech platforms from forcing other companies, the restaurants or the tea shops, from selling their products at a cheaper price. So this could help, but it is going to take some time. But you know, there's another side to all of this competition in China. Number one, it can produce very, very competitive companies. So you see this in the auto industry, where some companies have become incredibly competitive, so much so that auto firms overseas really can't compete with them anymore. You know, there's another aspect of this, though, that I think the central government is thinking about, and that's employment. Urban youth unemployment has been very high over the past couple of years. And a lot of young people don't want to work in factories anymore. So delivery driving is a perfect occupation for these young people that are coming into cities from the countryside. And I don't think the government wants that right now. So it's possible that this will continue at some level for quite a long time. Don, thanks as ever for joining us. Thank you. For 10 days every year, the state of Florida hosts the annual Python Challenge. The Python Challenge occurs in the Everglades. What happens is about 900 people descend on the park to try and cull snakes. They got hunting during the dead of night when the pythons leave their nests to forage. Rebecca Jackson is our southern correspondent. It's the time of year when the Everglades is stickiest and hottest and buggiest. So in terms of the competitors, it sounds like it's a little bit of everything. This is like the ultimate event for the Florida man and clearly the Florida woman. So one of the organizers of the challenge told me that this is not a bloodlust for pythons. In fact, it's a conservation project. Pythons were brought to Florida in the 1970s as exotic pets. But eventually, they grew too big to be good housemates, and they were released or escaped into the state park. There, they bred rapidly and ate up basically all of the natural wildlife. They can come to be up to 20 feet long, and that actually means that they can strangle and eat an alligator. The pythons are responsible for a 95% drop in fur animals in the park. So these are deer and otters. Because the snake has no natural predator, people had to step in. But the scale of the problem you're describing doesn't really match up to the numbers of snakes caught during the challenge. That's a good point. The Python Challenge is basically a giant ad for their year-long conservation initiative. The Florida Fish and Wildlife Conservation Commission and the South Florida Water Management District teamed up to create the Python Elimination Program. Together, they employ 100 year-round contractors. Since 2017, their hunters have removed 16,000 snakes from the Everglades. The woman that I went out python hunting with is one of those contractors. Her name is Chris Bartish, and she's a biologist who works for the state and hunts pythons five nights a week. She cares deeply about the animals and about the mission. And because of that, she says she finds the job actually kind of addictive. She said that going out to hunt pythons for a good cause is like an Easter egg hunt for adults. So, like, it's an actual job for her. I mean, I feel like all the jobs I had in Florida were really boring by comparison. She does do this as a full-time job, and I can't say that it's not boring, necessarily. We were out there for eight or nine hours overnight, and let me tell you, it's kind of slow-moving. But certainly, when you catch a python, there's a thrill to it. But this is also partly about the money, right? Contractors get $50 for the first four feet of a snake and $25 for every foot after that. And some decide to sell their carcasses to companies that make the skins into handbags and jewelry. And those companies are working on making the meat into dog food. A man named Dusty Crumb, who describes himself as the Python Wildman, sells python fat soap out of his pizza parlor on the edge of the park. This group of contractors is a pretty unusual bunch, and some of them are passionate about teaching the next generation to do what they do. After the big python challenge, there was a youth hunt. In the early months after the fall of former dictator Bashar al-Assad, Syria was euphoric. And I think what you've started to see over the summer months is the first sort of emergence of opposition to Ahmad al-Shara's new government in Damascus. And I think that's a key thing that he hasn't managed to do yet. And how does all of this play out for the ordinary person trying to live their life? I think when the US administration, Donald Trump, lifted sanctions on Syria a few months back, and we've had this wave of foreign companies from the Gulf and from Turkey coming to Syria and signing these big deals. For a couple that has divorced three times, Uchiyama Yukari and Koike Yukio get along surprisingly well. Moeka Iida writes about Japan for The Economist. In May, for the first time in nearly three decades, politicians debated on this issue. Opposition parties submitted bills that would have allowed more flexibility to the system. Law doesn't officially say that women have to take the husband's name. But that's what happens in 95% of cases. To be clear, I'm not suggesting this is the main reason why the birth rate is falling in Japan. But I do think the surname law does have an impact on demographics as well.
